L52: ## 1 Executive Summary
L53: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L54: Tafasitamab is an Fc-modified antibody that binds to CD19 antigen expressed on the surface of pre-B and mature B lymphocytes and on several B-cell malignancies, including diffuse large B-cell lymphoma. Upon binding to CD19, tafasitamab mediates B-cell lysis through apoptosis and immune effector mechanisms, including ADCC and ADCP.
L55: The recommended dose of tafasitamab is 12 mg/kg as an intravenous infusion given according to the following schedule:
L56: - Cycle 1: Days 1, 4, 8, 15 and 22 of the 28-day cycle
- Cycle 2 and 3: Days 1, 8, 15 and 22 of each 28-day cycle
- Cycle 4 and beyond: Days 1 and 15 of each 28-day cycle
L57: Tafasitamab is administered in combination with lenalidomide for a maximum of 12 cycles and then tafasitamab is continued as monotherapy until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.
L58: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L59: The review team recommends accelerated approval of tafasitamab in combination with lenalidomide for:
L60: “The treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL) not otherwise specified, including DLBCL arising from low grade lymphoma, and who are not eligible for autologous stem cell transplant (ASCT).”
L61: This recommendation is based on a favorable benefit risk profile from trial, MOR208C203, a multicenter single arm trial of tafasitamab at the proposed dose and schedule in combination with lenalidomide 25 mg administered for up to 12 cycles in patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL.
L62: Trial MOR208C203 (L-MIND) enrolled 81 patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL based on local pathology. Patients had relapsed or refractory DLBCL after 1 to 3 prior systemic therapies, including a CD20-directed cytolytic antibody, and were not candidates for high dose chemotherapy (HDC) followed by ASCT. The primary efficacy population was a total of 71 patients who had confirmed DLBCL based on central pathology. The median age was 71 years (range 41 to 86); 55% were male, 95% were White and 3% were Asian. The median number of prior therapies was two; 49% had one prior line of treatment, and 51% had 2 to 4 prior lines. Efficacy was established based on the best overall response rate (ORR), defined as the
L63: proportion of patients with a complete or partial response assessed by an Independent Review Committee (IRC) using the International Working Group Response Criteria (Cheson 2007) supported by the duration of response. The best overall response rate was 55% (95% CI 43%, 67%); 37% of patients had a complete response and 18% of patients had a partial response. The median duration of response was 21.7 months (range-0-24 months).
L64: The most common adverse reactions (\ge 20\%) were neutropenia, fatigue, anemia, diarrhea, thrombocytopenia, cough, pyrexia, peripheral edema, respiratory tract infection, and decreased appetite. Important safety risks include infusion related reactions, myelosuppression and infections. Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 5% of patients. The rates of permanent treatment discontinuation of tafasitamab due to adverse reactions was 15%.
L65: Trial MOR208C201 provided evidence to support the contribution of tafasitamab to the treatment effect in patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL. The ORR in the 35 patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL enrolled on MOR208C201 who received single agent tafasitamab was 26% (95% CI: 13%, 43%). The ORR results of the L-MIND study were also considered in the context of an estimated ORR of 32% (95% CI: 21%, 43%) for single agent lenalidomide in Study MOR208C206 (RE-MIND), a retrospective observational cohort study evaluating lenalidomide monotherapy using real world data, and literature reports regarding the activity of single agent lenalidomide. However, there were several limitations to the observational study which render formal statistical comparisons unfeasible.
L66: The uncertainty of the magnitude of the treatment effect with the addition of tafasitamab is a concern due to the small sample size. However, the observed depth of response, overall response, and response duration are clinically meaningful. These efficacy data provide substantial evidence of effectiveness and are the basis for the recommended accelerated approval. The approved indication will reflect the subtypes of DLBCL enrolled on the L-MIND trial; DLBCL not otherwise specified including DLBCL arising from low grade lymphoma. A Post-marketing requirement to confirm the benefit of tafasitamab in patients with [REDACTED] (b)(4) DLBCL in a randomized controlled trial will be issued. A post marketing commitment will be issued to evaluate the efficacy and safety of tafasitamab in underrepresented racial subgroups.
L67: ## 1.3.Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L68: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L69: Tafasitamab is an Fc-modified antibody that binds to CD19 antigen expressed on the surface of pre-B and mature B lymphocytes and on several B-cell malignancies, including diffuse large B-cell lymphoma. Upon binding to CD19, tafasitamab mediates B-cell lysis through apoptosis and immune effector mechanisms, including ADCC and ADCP.
L70: **Efficacy:** Efficacy of tafasitamab in patients with relapsed or refractory diffuse large B cell lymphoma (DLBCL) is based on results from a single, multicenter, single arm trial, MOR208C203 (L-MIND) in 71 patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL confirmed by central pathology review. The median number of prior therapies was two; 49% had one prior line of treatment, and 51% had 2 to 4 prior lines. The primary reasons patients were not candidates for autologous stem cell transplantation (ASCT) included age (47%), refractoriness to salvage chemotherapy (27%), comorbidities (13%) and refusal of high dose chemotherapy/ASCT (13%). Race was collected in 92% of these patients and of these, 95% were white and 3% were Asian.
L71: Patients received tafasitamab 12 mg/kg in combination with lenalidomide 25 mg for 12 cycles given on days 1-21 of a 28 day cycle followed by tafasitamab monotherapy. Tafasitamab was administered according to the following schedule:
L72: - Cycle 1: Days 1, 4, 8, 15 and 22 of the 28-day cycle
- Cycle 2 and 3: Days 1, 8, 15 and 22 of each 28-day cycle
- Cycle 4 and beyond: Days 1 and 15 of each 28-day cycle
L73: Efficacy was established based on best overall response rate (ORR) assessed by an Independent Review Committee (IRC) using the International Working Group Response Criteria (Cheson 2007) and duration of response. The best overall response rate was 55% [95% CI (43%, 67%)] with a duration of response of 21.7 months (range 0, 24). The complete response (CR) rate was 37%.
L74: The contribution of tafasitamab to the activity in the combination was informed by the efficacy data from MOR208C201, a single arm trial evaluating single-agent tafasitamab in patients with relapsed or refractory non-Hodgkin's Lymphoma. The ORR in the 35 patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL enrolled on MOR208C201 who received single agent tafasitamab was 26.0%(95% CI: 13%, 43%). The efficacy data from the L-MIND trial was considered in the context of the estimated ORR of 32% noted with single
L75: agent lenalidomide in the MOR208C206 Study (RE-MIND), a retrospective observational cohort study evaluating lenalidomide monotherapy using real world data, and literature reports regarding the activity of single agent lenalidomide. However, there were several limitations to the observational data and formal comparisons to the data from RE-MIND were not made.
L76: **Safety:** Tafasitamab demonstrated an acceptable safety profile for the patient population. L-MIND served as the primary trial for the safety evaluation, this was supported by a pooled analysis of three trials evaluating tafasitamab as monotherapy. Important safety risks include infusion related reactions, myelosuppression and infections. Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 5% of patients. The rates of permanent treatment discontinuation of tafasitamab due to adverse reactions was 15%.
L77: **Benefit-Risk:** The available data provides evidence of the benefit risk of tafasitamab in combination with lenalidomide for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL not otherwise specified, including DLBCL arising from low grade lymphoma, and who are not eligible for ASCT. The intended population has very few, if any, treatment options, and toxicities with tafasitamab and lenalidomide combination tended to be manageable with supportive care and dose modifications. The risks and the benefit/risk profile are acceptable for the intended population. The final patient population will reflect the types of DLBCL lymphoma enrolled, including DLBCL not otherwise specified and DLBCL arising from low grade lymphoma.
L78: The limitations of the data are 1) the uncertainty of the magnitude of effect due to the small sample size and lack of a comparator arm 2) the limitations of the retrospective observational data referenced, 3) and the data collected on time-to-event endpoints are not interpretable, therefore, it cannot be determined if PFS or OS are prolonged. A post-marketing requirement to confirm the benefit of tafasitamab in patients with [REDACTED] (b) (4) DLBCL in a randomized controlled trial will be issued. A post-marketing commitment will be issued to evaluate the efficacy and safety of tafasitamab in underrepresented racial subgroups.
L79: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Relapsed or refractory diffuse large B cell lymphoma (DLBCL) is fatal if not cured. In relapsed or refractory DLBCL, standard salvage regimens produce ORRs of 20-30%, with <15% CR and an estimated median OS of 6 months.	• Relapsed or refractory DLBCL remains an incurable disease.
L80: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current Treatment Options	High dose therapy followed by autologous stem cell transplantation (ASCT) remains the standard of care for relapsed/refractory patients with DLBCL if eligible. However, may patients may be ineligible for transplant either due to age or comorbidity or refractory disease.
Widely used less intensive regimens include gemcitabine, bendamustine and lenalidomide, but there is no standard of care and response rates are not durable.
Axicabtagene ciloleucel and tisagenlecleucel have regular approval and selinexor and polotuzumab vedotin have accelerated approval for the treatment of relapsed or refractory large B-cell lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy.
There is no FDA-approved therapy for patients with DLBCL after failure of one prior therapy or for patients who are ineligible for autologous stem cell transplantation.
Patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL who are ineligible for autologous stem cell transplant have limited treatment options and is a population with unmet medical need.

Benefit	In a single arm-trial of tafasitamab in combination with lenalidomide for patients with a diagnosis of relapsed or refractory DLBCL and not eligible for autologous stem cell transplantation, the Independent review committee (IRC) assessed best overall response rate (ORR) in 71 patients with DLBCL confirmed by central pathology was 55% (95% CI 43%, 67%); 37% of patients had a complete response. The median duration of response (DOR) was 21.7 months (range, 0-24 months).
The ORR in the 35 patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL enrolled on MOR208C201 who received single agent tafasitamab was 26.0% and provides supportive evidence of efficacy of single agent tafasitamab.
The efficacy data from the L-MIND trial was considered in the
Based on the ORR and durable DOR, tafasitamab in combination with lenalidomide has meaningful treatment benefit in the intended patient population with relapsed or refractory DLBCL.
A post marketing requirement to confirm the benefit of tafasitamab in patients with DLBCL will be issued.
L81: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
context of the ORR of 32% noted with single agent lenalidomide in the RE-MIND retrospective observational study and literature data.	
Risk and Risk Management	The 81 patients enrolled and treated with tafasitamab at the intended dose and regimen in the L-MIND trial are the primary safety population.
A total of 46 patients (56.8%) were exposed to tafasitamab for > 6 months, a total of 34 patients (42.0%) were exposed for greater than 1 year and a total of 19 patients (23.5%) were exposed for > 2 years.
Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 5% of patients. The rates of permanent treatment discontinuation of tafasitamab due to adverse reactions was 15%.
Important safety risks include infusion related reactions, myelosuppression and infections.
TEAEs that occurred in more than 20% of patients on the L-MIND trial included neutropenia, fatigue, anemia, diarrhea, thrombocytopenia, cough, peripheral edema, pyrexia, respiratory tract infection, and decreased appetite.
Tafasitamab+ lenalidomide has an acceptable safety profile in the intended population.
Toxicities were manageable with appropriate treatment interruption and discontinuation.
Infusion-related reaction, myelosuppression, and serious or opportunistic infections will be included in the Warnings and Precautions.
The USPI includes toxicity management guidelines for hematologic and non-hematologic adverse reactions.
The safe use of tafasitamab and lenalidomide can be managed through labeling and routine oncology care.

L86: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L87: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L88: ## The Applicant's Position:
L89: Non-Hodgkin lymphomas (NHLs) account for approximately 5% of all new cancer cases and comprise a heterogeneous group of lymphoproliferative malignancies, which in 90% of cases are derived from B-cells. Diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL), the most common high-grade NHL, accounts for approximately 40% of NHLs (Armitage and Weisenburger, 1998; Smith 2015; Swerdlow et al., 2008) and comprises 60% of all new lymphomas in the elderly population (Thieblemont and Coiffier, 2007). According to data from the United States (U.S.) Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) program, it is estimated that in 2019, there were 74,200 new NHL cases in the U.S., which constitute approximately 4.2% of all new cancer cases. Additionally, there were an estimated 19,970 deaths from NHL in 2019 (https://seer.cancer.gov/statfacts/html/nhl.html). The median age at the diagnosis of DLBCL is 64 years, and the majority of patients present with advanced disease.
L90: The standard of care for first-line therapy for DLBCL regardless of stage is R-CHOP; however, approximately 30% of patients relapse despite having achieved complete remission (CR), and a further 20% of patients are refractory to R-CHOP (Coiffier and Sarkozy 2016; Sehn and Gascoyne, 2015). Patients with disease that has relapsed or is refractory to R-CHOP have a poor prognosis with short survival times, ranging 6-12 months (Coiffier and Sarkozy, 2016; Smith et al., 2015, Crump et al 2017, Farooq et al., 2017). For the minority of patients who are fit for high-intensity therapy, it may be possible to achieve cure using intensive re-induction immunochemotherapy, followed by high-dose chemotherapy (HDC) with subsequent autologous stem cell transplantation (ASCT). In transplant-eligible R/R DLBCL patients, cure is achieved in approximately 10% of cases. The remaining 90% will eventually relapse again and succumb to their disease (Van Den Neste et al., 2016).
L91: The majority of patients with relapsed/refractory (R/R) DLBCL are not eligible for intensive immunochemotherapy and ASCT, generally due to advanced age or co-morbidities. The NCCN guidelines recommend that these or patients who relapse after ASCT participate in a clinical trial or receive third-line systemic therapies, palliative involved-site radiotherapy or best supportive care treatments, indicating a lack of effective treatment options. The development of novel therapies is required due to the continued unmet medical need in patients with R/R DLBCL.
L92: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L93: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment above.
L94: ## 2.2 Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L95: R-CHOP is the standard of care for all newly-diagnosed patients; approximately 40% of patients will ultimately relapse or progress (Vitolo et al., 2017). In patients whose disease progresses or relapses after first-line treatment, salvage chemotherapy options such as DHAP (cisplatin, cytarabine, dexamethasone), DHAX (dexamethasone, cytarabine, oxaliplatin), ICE (ifosfamide, carboplatin, etoposide), or GDP (gemcitabine, dexamethasone, cisplatin/carboplatin), with or without the anti-CD20 mAb rituximab followed by HDC with ASCT offer the potential for cure. HDC/ASCT significantly improves disease-free survival and overall survival (OS) in patients with relapsed / refractory DLBCL (Gisselbrecht et al., 2010; Philip et al., 1995).
L96: However, HDC/ASCT may not be appropriate, especially in patients who are chemorefractory, have comorbidities or are older, and this applies to a significant proportion of patients with R/R DLBCL. Approximately 25% of patients with R/R DLBCL undergo HDC/ASCT. For the remaining patient population, several second-line combination therapies such as B-R, R-GemOx, R-Lenalidomide and other therapies as per NCCN guidelines are used with variable success.
L97: Recently, three new therapies have been approved for DLBCL patients who have progressed after second-line therapy. These comprise two chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cell therapies (CAR-T therapies), axicabtagene ciloleucel (Yescarta™) and tisagenlecleucel (Kymriah™) as well as the antibody-drug conjugate polatuzumab vedotin, in combination with bendamustine and rituximab (Pola + BR). Both CAR-T cell therapies target CD19, the same antigen as tafasitamab, whereas polatuzumab vedotin targets the CD79b antigen.
L98: **Table 1: FDA-Approved Therapy for Relapsed/Refractory DLBCL**

L103: ## The Applicant's Position:
L104: The CAR-T cell therapies may produce durable CRs, but their use has limitations because of the toxicity profile, which requires rigorous selection criteria and inpatient administration, and restricted availability in tertiary care facilities. Additionally, the waiting period associated with CAR-T manufacture may be prohibitive in patients with symptomatic or rapidly progressing disease. Polatuzumab vedotin received accelerated approval for use in combination with bendamustine and rituximab (pola-BR). All three recently approved products are licensed for use in the third-line setting (after failure of two or more prior systemic therapies). There is no FDA-approved therapy for patients with R/R DLBCL after failure of one prior therapy, or for patients who are unable to receive CAR-T therapy.
L105: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L106: There is no FDA approved therapy for patients with R/R DLBCL after one prior therapy or ineligible for autologous stem cell transplant. FDA also agrees that although these CAR-T cell therapies can produce durable CRs, their use has limitations in the general R/R DLBCL population. FDA also notes that other combination salvage regimens include platinum, etoposide, and/or cytarabine based combination regimens and gemcitabine-based regimens. FDA notes the following:
L107: The proposed intended population for tafasitamab is different than the populations for tisagenlecleucel and axicabtagene ciloleucel mentioned by the Applicant in Table 1. The proposed indication for tafasitamab is in combination with lenalidomide followed by MONJUVI monotherapy for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL, including DLBCL arising from low grade lymphoma, and who are not eligible for, (b)(4) ASCT usually due to advanced age or co-morbidities. The intended population for tafasitamab and polatuzumab vedotin are similar, as trials supporting the approval of drugs have enrolled patients not a candidate for SCT, however polatuzumab vedotin received accelerated approval in combination with bendamustine and a rituximab product for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma, not otherwise specified, after at least two prior therapies. Additionally, selinexor received accelerated approval in June 2020, for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (RR DLBCL), not otherwise specified, who have received at least 2 prior therapies. The IRC assessed ORR was 29% (95% CI: 22, 38) and CR rate was 13% according to Lugano criteria

L121: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L122: Relevant discussions from prior meetings are summarized below:
L123: ## Type C Meeting December 2, 2014
L124: The focus if this meeting was a randomized trial comparing tafasitamab + bendamustine versus rituximab + bendamustine. The population proposed for this trial was patients who are not eligible for intensive therapy including SCT. It was discussed that patients who are eligible for standard therapy but are unwilling or refuse to undergo SCT should not be included in a registrational trial. The Agency had concerns with the dosing regimen and recommended before beginning a planned pivotal trial that integrated dose-response and exposure response analyses should occur.
L125: ## Type A Meeting August 10, 2015
L126: This meeting was to discuss non-agreement on a Special Protocol Assessment for a protocol of titled "A Phase III, Randomized, Multicenter Study of MOR00208 with Bendamustine versus Rituximab with Bendamustine in Patients with Relapsed or Refractory Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma (R-R DLBCL) Who Are Not Eligible for High-Dose Chemotherapy (HDC) and Autologous Stem-Cell Transplantation (ASCT). While this is not the trial submitted in support of this Application, some of the discuss that occurred is relevant and summarized below:
L127: - At this time, the Agency did not agree with a dose of 12 mg/kg as an optimal dose for single agent use as an integrated dose-response analysis for safety and activity to should be done. It was discussed the Applicant should investigate the trend of exposure-weight relationship in order to determine whether weight based dosing or flat dosing was the optimal dosing regimen.
- Regarding the proposed criterion for ineligibility for SCT it was discussed that age &gt;60 is not a contraindication to SCT. It was also discussed that some criteria assessed by the treating physician are nondescript and would be problematic for labeling.
L128: ## Type C Meeting June 28, 2017
L129: This meeting occurred to discuss a Special Protocol Assessment of the trial entitled "A Phase III, Randomized, Multicenter Study of MOR00208 with Bendamustine versus Rituximab with
L130: Bendamustine in Patients with Relapsed or Refractory Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma Who Are Not Eligible for High-Dose Chemotherapy (HDC) and Autologous Stem-Cell-Transplantation (ASCT)". During this meeting the Agency expressed concerns regarding continued monotherapy treatment with monoclonal antibodies tafasitamab or rituximab in patients with DLBCL. The Agency suggested a second randomization prior to maintenance.
L131: ## Type B Meeting January 24, 2018
L132: The Applicant requested this meeting to discuss further development and filing for tafasitamab. At this time the Agency had concerns regarding an initial application based on the L-MIND trial, a single arm trial that evaluates tafasitamab in combination with lenalidomide. For approval, the Applicant was informed evidence of the activity of single agent tafasitamab would be needed, as the contribution of tafasitamab to the efficacy of the combination could not be determined in the L-MIND trial. Also following concerns were also mentioned:
L133: - Time to event endpoints such as PFS, TTP, and OS are not interpretable from single arm trials.
- Drugs granted accelerated approval must meet the same statutory standards for safety and effectiveness as those granted traditional approval.
L134: ## Type B Meeting August 1, 2018
L135: The purpose of this meeting was to discuss further clinical development and filing strategy. At this meeting, the Applicant notified the Agency of the strategy to compare the combination of tafasitamab and lenalidomide to lenalidomide monotherapy by a comparative network meta-analysis with published data of clinical trials with lenalidomide monotherapy in R/R DLBCL and to compare the combination of tafasitamab and lenalidomide with all published data of regimens listed in NCCN guidelines for the treatment of patients with r/r DLBCL not eligible for SCT. The Agency did not agree with this approach. The Agency reiterated the need for person level data for patients in the historical control. The Agency also stressed the importance of measuring endpoints the same way between patients in L-MIND and the RWE patients.
L136: ## Type C Meeting September 12, 2018
L137: This meeting was to discuss a planned protocol amendment for clinical study MOR208C204 entitled, "A Phase III, randomized, multicenter study of MOR00208 with Bendamustine versus Rituximab with Bendamustine in patients with relapsed or refractory Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma (R-R DLBCL) who are not eligible for High-Dose Chemotherapy (HDC) and Autologous Stem-Cell Transplantation (ASCT)". This meeting was focused on a trial not submitted with this Application, NKCC high vs NKCC low testing, co primary endpoints and hierarchical testing.
L138: This meeting was focused on the use of Real World Data included with this submission. During this meeting the following concerns were discussed:
L139: - The data quality from the L-MIND study may differ from the lenalidomide monotherapy cohort. There may be differences in type of data, missing data and different follow up. The Applicant was instructed to provide summary data such as the amount of missing data and duration of follow up to demonstrate comparability. For each data element the Applicant should provide evidence that the data can be captured adequately, accurately and non-differentially.
- The lenalidomide monotherapy cohort should resemble patients in the L-MIND study. It was recommended a matching approach be used rather than the overlap weights approach. The lenalidomide monotherapy cohort should be induced by matching patients from the database to patients in the L-MIND study. The Agency acknowledged that this could lead to sample size attrition. However, the Applicant was informed that substantial attrition is indicative of two very different populations, such that, it may not be reasonable to compare them.
- The concern of covariates, measured or unmeasured, may affect the outcome was also discussed. The Applicant was informed that when conducting observational studies, matching on as many reasonable covariates should occur. The Applicant had listed age, Ann-Arbor stage, IPI score, refractoriness to previous line of therapy, number of prior lines of therapy, history of primary refractoriness, and prior ASCT. It was mentioned other covariates such as laboratory values prior to index date should be included.
- The Applicant was informed at this time the Agency does not accept imputation of missing covariates for the purpose of estimating propensity scores.
- Concern regarding the collection of response data for assessment of the ORR endpoint was raised. Data from the clinical notes alone may not allow assessment of different aspects of response criteria. It was recommended the Applicant consider a validation study in patients who have clinical notes and imaging to ensure robustness of the data.
- Outcome misclassification was also discussed. Is a reported response an actual response? Is a reported lack of response an actual lack of response? The response rate in the lenalidomide monotherapy cohort may not be identifiable as it can be contaminated by the misclassification process. The Applicant was also informed that misclassification can affect covariates such as medical history, disease, staging, prior therapies at the time of the lenalidomide monotherapy index date. It was critical that values at the time of the index date were accurately captured.
- For an accurate assessment of the endpoints a minimum duration of follow up of 6 months for each patient included in the monotherapy cohort was recommended.
- Only patients with an initial dose of 25 mg of lenalidomide at the index date in the lenalidomide monotherapy cohort should be included.
L140: - The sponsor expressed concerns regarding the exclusion of patients with missing baseline covariates, which the Agency acknowledged, however, it was discussed that the goal is to learn about lenalidomide monotherapy activity in patients whose characteristics closely resemble those in the L-MIND trial.
- The Agency had concerns regarding the ability to capture duration of response in the external databases.
L141: Type C Meeting February 4, 2019
L142: This meeting was to obtain agreement of the biomarker drives protocol amendment of the ongoing B-MIND trial. This trial is currently ongoing and not submitted with this application.

L168: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L169: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L170: Tafasitamab (also known as MOR00208, MOR208, and XmAb5574) is a humanized IgG1/2 hybrid Fc-modified monoclonal antibody that targets CD19, a cell surface protein expressed on B cells and follicular dendritic cells of the hematopoietic system and on several B-cell malignancies including DLBCL1,2.
L171: Tafasitamab bound to human CD19 antigen with an average dissociation constant (K_D) of approximately 0.4 nM. Comparable binding of tafasitamab was observed to CD19-expressing lymphocytes of human, cynomolgus monkey, and rhesus monkey, and low-affinity binding to dog, rabbit, rat, and mouse lymphocytes. Tafasitamab exhibited similar binding affinities to human and monkey Fc gamma receptor (Fc\gammaRs). There was no off-target binding of tafasitamab detected in tissue panels from the human and cynomolgus monkey. Tafasitamab bound to mononuclear leukocytes in the majority of tissues examined, lymphocytes in blood, and hematopoietic precursor cells in the bone marrow in both species. Based on these findings, the cynomolgus monkey was chosen as the relevant toxicology species.
L172: The in vitro activity of tafasitamab was assessed in human B-cell lines, as well as primary cells derived from patients with hematological malignancies. Upon binding to CD19, tafasitamab mediates B-cell lysis of tumor cells through apoptosis, mainly antibody dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC), and antibody-dependent cell-mediated phagocytosis (ADCP). Tafasitamab showed ADCC activity in several CD19+ lymphoma cell lines including DLBCL; with NK-mediated lysis of 25 to 90% of lymphoma cells, a half-maximal concentration (EC_{50}) values of 1 to 10 ng/mL was observed. Tafasitamab induced macrophage-mediated ADCP in CD19+ ALL cell lines with a maximal phagocytosis of 30-40% at tafasitamab concentrations of 1 to 10 ng/mL. In in vitro studies, treatment of DLBCL cells with the combination of tafasitamab and lenalidomide resulted in increased ADCC activity compared to tafasitamab or lenalidomide alone. In the absence of effector cells, tafasitamab induced apoptosis in DLBCL cells (SU-DHL-6). The apoptotic activity was greater with tafasitamab compared to control antibodies containing the same CD19-binding domain but non-modified Fc domain (anti-CD19 IgG1 analog) or Fc-knockout (anti-CD19 Fc-KO). The in vivo anti-tumor activity of tafasitamab at doses ranging from 1 to 10 mg/kg was demonstrated in xenograft models bearing human lymphoma tumors.
L173: The safety of tafasitamab was evaluated in GLP-compliant 8- and 13-week general toxicology studies in cynomolgus monkeys. Monkeys received tafasitamab by intravenous (i.v.) infusion
L174: 1 Tedder, T. F., Isaacs, C. M. Isolation of cDNAs encoding the CD19 antigen of human and mouse B lymphocytes: a new member of the immunoglobulin superfamily. J. Immun. 143: 712-717, 1989.
L175: 2 Olejniczak SH et al 2006. A quantitative exploration of surface antigen expression in common B-cell malignancies using flow cytometry: Immunol Invest. 2006;35(1):93-114.
L176: once every two weeks at 2, 10 or 50 mg/kg in the 8-week study, or once every week at 0, 10, 30 or 100 mg/kg in the 13-week study. Monkeys were observed for 90 days and 132 days for recovery following the dosing periods in the 8-week and 13-week studies, respectively. In the 8-week study, tafasitamab caused marked B-cell depletion at all doses tested, consistent with its pharmacological activity; the effects were reversible. Decreases in B-cell were associated with decreased white blood cell counts and absolute lymphocytes, decreased spleen weights, and altered spleen histology (decreased cellularity of the splenic germinal center). ADAs were detected in 47% of tafasitamab-treated monkeys, resulting in enhanced clearance of tafasitamab in the 8-week study. In the 13-week toxicology study, reductions in lymphocytes, WBC, and neutrophils were less pronounced compared to the results observed in the 8-week study. The results may suggest presence of neutralizing ADAs that were not detected due to high circulating tafasitamab levels. In the 13-week study, neurological behavioral changes including tremors and changes in body temperature were observed. The number of animals affected and the number of incidences of the tremors were higher at the high dose. The tremors resolved following the termination of dosing. Statistically significant differences in body temperature (100-102°F) were observed mainly at 100 mg/kg during dosing that recovered in the recovery period. There were no effects on menstrual cycle length in females and no histopathology/ microscopic changes in male and female reproductive organs. Mean maximum serum concentration (C_{max} observed after the 13th dose) and exposure (AUC_{0-144h}) in cynomolgus monkeys dosed at 100 mg/kg in the 13-week study were 4595 \mug/mL and 8375 \mug\cdotday/mL, respectively. This is approximately 8 to 9-fold the corresponding values in humans observed in clinical study (Study No. MOR208C201) with weekly dosing at 12 mg/kg (mean C_{max} = 547.4 \mug/mL; Mean AUC_{0-last} = 914.3 \mug\cdotday/mL).
L177: Based on a prior agreement with the FDA, the Applicant did not conduct reproductive and developmental toxicity studies with tafasitamab since tafasitamab is administered in combination with lenalidomide, a known teratogen and embryo-fetal toxicant. The Applicant submitted other information in the weight-of-evidence (WOE) reproductive risk assessment on the potential effects of tafasitamab (monotherapy) on embryofetal development, consistent with the Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations Guidance for Industry. Based on the WOE risk assessment, treatment of pregnant women with tafasitamab may result in depletion of B cells and thus dysfunction of immune response in a newborn. This risk has been communicated in the tafasitamab label. Normal B-cell counts and immunologic function may be restored based on reversibility of effects in animal studies. The contraception duration listed in the label is until at least 3 months after the last dose and covers a period of at least 5 half-lives for tafasitamab (T_{1/2}= 17 days). Additionally, lactating women are advised not to breastfeed during treatment and for 3 months after the last dose.
L178: Carcinogenicity studies are not necessary to support the marketing of therapeutics intended to treat patients with advanced cancer as discussed in the ICH S9 guidance document.
L179: Genotoxicity studies are generally not necessary to support marketing of biotechnology-derived pharmaceuticals such as tafasitamab as discussed in the ICH S6 guidance document.
L180: The nonclinical studies submitted to this BLA provide sufficient information to support the use of tafasitamab (Monjuvi), in combination with lenalidomide, for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL). From the nonclinical pharmacology and toxicology perspective, tafasitamab (Monjuvi) is recommended for approval.
L181: ## 5.2.Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L182: ## The Applicant's Position:
L183: There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology for tafasitamab.
L184: ## 5.3.Pharmacology
L185: ## Primary Pharmacology
L186: The FDA agrees with the primary pharmacology data summarized by the Applicant that describes tafasitamab's pharmacological effects including ADCC, ADCP activities, apoptosis of tumor cells and the increased anti-tumor activities in combination with lenalidomide. Tafasitamab does not mediate complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC).
L187: Binding affinity of tafasitamab to human CD19 was measured in cell-based formats (study #TR07098). Tafasitamab bound to CD19-expressing human embryonic kidney (HEK) 293 cells transfected with a human CD19 expression plasmid in a concentration-dependent manner with an observed half-maximal concentration (EC_{50}) of 0.72 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL}, or 4.8 \text{ nM}. CD19-expressing human tumor cell lines (Raji and RS4;11), were used to determine the steady-state cell binding by measuring the equilibrium dissociation constant (K_D) by flow cytometry. The steady-state cell binding assays indicated that tafasitamab binds cell-surface CD19 in the range of 0.29 to 0.46 \text{ nM}, resulting in an average K_D of approximately 0.4 \text{ nM}.
L188: Binding of tafasitamab to CD19-expressing lymphocytes from multiple mammalian species was assessed to identify the relevant species for toxicological evaluation (study #XC9-23209). Whole blood samples from human donors, cynomolgus and rhesus monkeys, dogs, rabbits, rats, and mice were evaluated in flow cytometric studies using fluorescein isothiocyanate conjugated (FITC) tafasitamab. Binding of tafasitamab-FITC to human, cynomolgus monkey and rhesus monkey lymphocytes was comparable. Tafasitamab showed lower binding to rat and mouse, and no binding to dog and rabbit lymphocytes.
L189: Tafasitamab binding characteristics to human, cynomolgus monkey, and mouse Fc\gammaRs were determined using a surface plasmon resonance (SPR)-based method (study # TR08137). Antibodies were immobilized on a protein A/G chip surface and injected with serially diluted recombinant Fc\gammaR proteins. The affinity of tafasitamab increased relative to IgG1 (native IgG1 control) for all Fc\gammaRs with the strongest increase observed for the Fc\gammaR\text{IIIa} variants for human and monkey. In addition, tafasitamab exhibited similar binding affinities to human and monkey Fc\gammaRs; the human-to-monkey fold difference in K_D was 0.4 for Fc\gammaR1, 1.1 for Fc\gammaR\text{IIIa}, 1.4 for Fc\gammaR\text{IIb}, and 2.5 for Fc\gammaR\text{IIIa}. These studies support the use of the cynomolgus monkey as a pharmacologically relevant test model.
L190: ## In vitro activity of Tafasitamab against tumor cell lines
L191: In vitro and in vivo pharmacology studies with tafasitamab used several controls antibodies (mAbs) for comparison purposes. See details in the table below.
L192: **Table 4: Control Antibodies Used in the Pharmacology Studies with Tafasitamab**
L193: Name	mAb	Characteristics
XENP5603 (also known as IgG1, 5603_IgG1, IgG1 analog, Xmab5603, non-Fc-enhanced Xmab5603)	Anti-CD19 IgG1	Possesses the same CD19 binding domain as tafasitamab but carries a non-engineered human IgG1 Fc-domain.
XENP6187 (also known as Fc-KO, 6187_KO)	Fc-Knockout	Possesses the same CD19 binding domain as tafasitamab, but carries G236R/L328R mutations in the human IgG1 Fc-domain, which are intended to abrogate interactions with Fc gamma receptors
XENP4614 (also known as IgG1 control, negative control IgG1 antibody)	XmAb isotype control	Possesses no binding to CD19 antigen
L194: ## Antiproliferation activity
L195: The antiproliferative and apoptosis properties of tafasitamab related to direct interaction between the target antigen, CD19, and the variable region (Fv) of tafasitamab were evaluated (Study # TR08138). These assays were carried out using DLBCL derived SU-DHL-6 cells in the presence of tafasitamab or control antibodies crosslinked by Fc\gammaR\text{IIIa}-GST. Secondary crosslinking of Fc\gammaR\text{IIIa}-GST tag was used to simulate in vivo effects, but there were no effector cells present in these assays (e.g., no PBMCs). The antiproliferative activity of tafasitamab (assessed by ATP levels) was compared with those of control antibodies, an anti-CD19 IgG1 analog (XENP5603 or IgG1), and an anti-CD19 Fc-KO (Fc-knockout; XENP6187) that knocks
L196: binding to Fc\gammaRs. These antibodies have the identical Fv region and equal affinity to CD19 binding, thus any differences in proliferation may represent differences in the Fc domain. In addition, an isotype XmAb control (XENP4614) was included as a negative control that does not bind to CD19. The crosslinked antibodies were incubated with SU-DHL-6 cells for 72 hours and cell titers were analyzed using a cell viability assay. Tafasitamab exhibited relatively higher antiproliferative activity compared to the control antibodies (IgG1 analog and Fc-KO), suggesting that modifications to the Fc domain may have contributed to this mode of action.
L197: ## Apoptosis
L198: Assay conditions were the same as described above for antiproliferative activity. Caspase activation and annexin V staining were used to detect apoptosis. The crosslinked antibodies and SU-DHL-6 cells were incubated for 48 hours. Caspase activity was quantitated using a fluorometric assay (Panel B in figure below). For the annexin V assay, cells were labeled with annexin V-PE and 7-amino-actinomycin D, and fluorescence was analyzed by flow cytometry.
L199: Tafasitamab exhibited relatively higher apoptosis activity compared to the control antibodies (IgG1 analog and Fc-KO). Since no effector cells were present, results suggest that antiproliferative activity may be through induction of apoptosis and modifications to the Fc domain. In this study, tafasitamab-induced apoptosis in a DLBCL cell line (SU-DHL-6) with a maximum percentage of caspase activity of ~34% and Annexin-V-positive cells of ~17%.
L200: **Figure 1: Binding of Tafasitamab and Its IgG1 and Fc-KO Analogs to CD19+ Cells**
L201: Binding of antibodies to CD19+ RS4;11 cells (ALL cell line) was tested by flow cytometry.
L202: **Figure 2: Anti-Proliferative Activity and Caspase-Mediated Apoptosis Activity of Tafasitamab**
L203: Studies were performed on SU-DHL-6 cells using a human Fc\gammaR\text{IIIa}-GST fusion receptor to provide antibody crosslinking. Cross-linked antibodies were used to assess both anti-proliferative and apoptotic activity of tafasitamab in comparison with control antibodies.
L204: ## Effector function
L205: The Fc-mediated immune effector function properties including ADCC, and ADCP activities of tafasitamab were evaluated in cell based assays in vitro against multiple CD19+ expressing tumor cell lines and primary tumors (Study # TR08139, MOR208L029).
L206: ## Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity
L207: For the ADCC assay (Study # TR08139), isolated interleukin-2 (IL-2)-activated natural killer (NK) cells were used as effector cells. Target cells included CD19+ tumor cell lines with varying levels of CD19 antigen (15,000 to 105,000 molecules/cell). XENP5603, an anti-CD19 IgG1, and XENP4614, an XmAb isotype, were used as control antibodies. Tumor cells were incubated with antibodies at different concentrations for opsonization. Effector cells and target were co-cultured and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) activity was measured using the cytotoxicity detection kit. Tafasitamab induced immune cell mediated ADCC (cell lysis) of 25% to 90% with no correlation with cell surface CD19 expression level; the EC50 ranged from 0.1 to 1 ng/mL. The ADCC effect was 100- to 1000-fold over the anti-CD19 IgG1 control antibody likely related to modifications made to Fc region of tafasitamab.
L208: Figure 3: NK Cell Mediated ADCC Activity of Tafasitamab (XmAb5574) Against Multiple Tumor Cell Lines
L209: The ADCC activity of the tafasitamab mediated by \gamma\delta T-cells (immune effector cells) derived from donors was also evaluated (Study #MOR208L029). T-cells (\gamma\delta) appear to recognize malignant cells, infiltrate tumors, and induce cytotoxic and pro-inflammatory activities3. T-cells (\gamma\delta) were isolated from donors and used as effector cells at different effector-to-target (E:T) cell ratios. Five lymphoma and leukemia cell lines as well as patient-derived (CLL, MCL and B-ALL) cells were used as target cells. Target cells were stained with carboxyfluorescein succinimidyl ester (CFSE) and treated with tafasitamab, Xmab5603 (non-Fc-modified antibody) or an IgG1 negative control antibody. Effector cells were added to the stained target cells and incubated for 4 hours. The lysis of target cells was determined using FACS analysis. Tafasitamab showed
L210: 3 Hoeres, T., et al., (2018): Improving the Efficiency of V\gamma9V\delta2 T-Cell Immunotherapy in Cancer. In Frontiers in immunology 9, p. 800.
L211: increased cell killing activity compared to control antibodies (tafasitamab &gt;Xmab5603&gt;IgG1 control) in five cell lines and in patient-derived cells.
L212: The in vitro ADCC of tafasitamab in combination with lenalidomide (LEN) against DLBCL cell lines was investigated (MOR208L039). The immunomodulatory drug lenalidomide activates T cells to release the cytokines interferon gamma (IFN-\gamma) and interleukin-2 (IL-2), which stimulate NK cell activity and lead to increased NK cell numbers4,5. Consequently, treatment in combination with lenalidomide appears to increase tafasitamab-mediated NK-cell activation and ADCC activity6.
L213: The antitumor activity of tafasitamab, LEN or the combination of both was determined with peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) isolated from healthy donors as effector cells and DLBCL cell lines (SU-DHL-2, -4, -6 and U-2932) as target cells. PBMCs were preincubated for 72 hours in the presence of 10 \muM LEN or DMSO (vehicle control). Subsequently, the preincubated cells were utilized in a FACS-based cytotoxicity assay in the presence or absence of tafasitamab to compare the cytotoxic effects of single agent or combination. Cytotoxicity was measured by quantification of dead and viable cells using the fluorescent DNA binding dye 4',6-diamidino-2-phenylindole (DAPI).
L214: Tafasitamab-mediated ADCC activity in DLBCL cells was increased after lenalidomide pre-treatment of PBMCs, resulting in an average increase of up to 10% (See table below) compared to LEN or tafasitamab alone. The results show that the combination of tafasitamab with lenalidomide resulted in increased cytotoxicity on DLBCL cell lines over single agent in vitro. Similar experiments conducted with NK cells isolated from six healthy donors showed enhancement of ADCC (\sim8%) for the combination (tafasitamab and LEN) compared to treatment with single-agent tafasitamab, in four out six donors.

L220: ## Phagocytosis
L221: CD14+ cell (macrophage) mediated phagocytosis (ADCP) was determined by flow cytometry (Study # TR08139). Monocyte derived macrophages were generated by cultivation of purified CD14+ monocytes from PBMCs in the presence of macrophage colony-stimulating factor (M-CSF). Two CD19 positive tumor cell lines, RS4;11 and SUP-B15, were labeled with the fluorescent dyes (PKH67 or CFSE). Tafasitamab, and control antibodies (IgG1 analog or XmAb isotype) at 0.1, 1, 10, and 100 ng/mL were added to the tumor cells and incubated with macrophages. Cells were then stained with fluorescent-labeled antibodies against macrophage cell surface-specific markers, anti-CD11b-APC and anti-CD14-APC. Phagocytosis was evaluated using flow cytometry with percent phagocytosis calculated as the number of PKH67 or CFSE
L222: (tumor cells)/APC (macrophages) double positive cells divided by the total number of tumor cells.
L223: Both tafasitamab and the IgG1 analog increased phagocytosis in a dose-dependent manner from 1 ng/mL; however, tafasitamab induced phagocytosis activity approximately 10-fold higher relative to the IgG1 analog.
L224: **Figure 4: ADCP Activity of Tafasitamab Against Tumor Cell Lines**
L225: ## Tafasitamab in vivo anti-tumor activity
L226: The anti-tumor activity of tafasitamab was demonstrated in severe combined immune deficiency mutation *Prkdc*scid (SCID) mice engrafted with human lymphoma cells (Raji, or RAMOS cells; study #s XC9-05407, XC9-05607, XC9-07607, and XC9-10008). Mice bearing subcutaneously injected Raji or RAMOS tumor cells given tafasitamab at 1, 3 or 10 mg/kg by intraperitoneal injection on Days 3, 6, 10 and 13 exhibited a statistically significant reduction in tumor growth compared to the PBS control group or XENP4614 (isotype control). In addition, at a dose of 10 mg/kg, 33% of the mice remained tumor-free.
L227: **Figure 5: Anti-Tumor Activity of Tafasitamab in SCID Mice Bearing Raji Cell Xenografts**
L228: Figure 6: Anti-Tumor Activity of Tafasitamab in SCID Mice Bearing RAMOS Xenografts
L229: The lifespan of animals after treatment with tafasitamab in combination with lenalidomide was evaluated in a disseminated RAMOS non-Hodgkin lymphoma survival model (Study #MOR208P009). RAMOS tumor cells were injected on Day 0 via tail vein injection to mice randomized into 6 groups (see table below for group designation). Starting on Day 3 after inoculation, tafasitamab was administered at 3 mg/kg by i.v. twice weekly for three weeks and lenalidomide (100 or 200 mg/kg) was administered by oral gavage daily for 28 consecutive days. For the combination treatment, lenalidomide/tafasitamab were administered at 100/3 or 200/3 mg/kg as described above. Vehicle control: Ora-Plus:Ora-Sweet SF (1:1) was administered orally for 28 days, and the isotype control antibody Ref_mAb_33 was administered i.v. twice weekly for three weeks. Following the completion of treatment, the animals were monitored for survival for five weeks post-inoculation. The lifespan was increased in the combination groups compared to the respective mono-therapy groups.

L232: ## The Applicant's Position:
L233: Tafasitamab is an Fc-enhanced monoclonal antibody that targets the CD19 antigen. Two amino acid modifications were introduced into the Fc-domain of tafasitamab leading to increased affinity to Fcy receptors, resulting in more potent immune effector cell mechanisms including enhanced ADCC and ADCP, compared with an antibody with an unmodified Fc-domain.
L234: In vitro assays demonstrated that upon binding to CD19, tafasitamab mediates B-cell lysis through: 1) engagement of immune effector cells, like NK cells, \gamma\delta T cells and phagocytes; and, 2) direct induction of cell death (apoptosis). Tafasitamab does not mediate CDC.
L235: The *in vivo* antitumor activity of tafasitamab monotherapy was evaluated using subcutaneous xenograft models in severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) mice engrafted with human Raji and RAMOS lymphoma cells. Tafasitamab treatment resulted in reduction in tumor growth.
L236: Lenalidomide exerts its antitumor activity by inhibition of proliferation and apoptosis as well via its immunostimulatory function to activate T- and NK cells (REVLIMID USPI). As NK cells are an important effector cell population for tafasitamab mediated ADCC, the combination of tafasitamab and lenalidomide was investigated preclinically. Increased anti-tumor activity in *in vitro* ADCC assays and increased survival in a xenograft *in vivo* model of disseminated RAMOS cells, compared with both monotherapy regimens, were observed. These data provided the rationale to investigate the combination of tafasitamab and lenalidomide in MOR208C203 (L-MIND).
L237: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L238: No secondary pharmacodynamic effect unrelated to CD19 binding were observed, thus no dedicated secondary pharmacodynamics studies have been conducted.
L239: Tafasitamab cross-reacts with CD19 on cynomolgus monkey B-lymphocytes. In cynomolgus monkeys, tafasitamab mediated B-cell depletion from peripheral blood as a secondary pharmacodynamic effect at doses ranging from 0.3 mg/kg to 100 mg/kg was observed. Histopathological changes to several lymphatic organs (decreased cellularity of the germinal centers in lymphoid follicles of spleen, mesenteric and mandibular lymph nodes) were observed. B-cell depletion in peripheral blood was reversible upon discontinuation of tafasitamab in cynomolgus monkeys.
L240: ## The Applicant's Position:
L241: The reduced peripheral B-cell counts and changes to lymphatic organs are considered exaggerated pharmacodynamic effects related to B-cell depletion.
L242: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L243: The FDA agrees with the Applicant that tafasitamab-related changes to lymphatic organs are exaggerated pharmacodynamic effects related to B-cell depletion. In the GLP tissue-cross reactivity studies with human and cynomolgus monkey tissues, tafasitamab binds only B-cells (as expected based on the expression profile of CD19).
L244: ## Safety Pharmacology
L245: ## The Applicant's Position:
L246: No stand-alone safety pharmacology studies were conducted. The safety pharmacology parameters were assessed within the pivotal 8-week and 13-week repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkey (XC-9-14408, MOR208P008) and did not reveal any unwanted effects on vital organ functions (cardiovascular, respiratory, central nervous system) at doses up to 100 mg/kg.
L247: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L248: The neurobehavioral assessments in the 13-week repeat-dose toxicology study in monkeys revealed severe tremors of the whole body observed occasionally in one out five females at the high dose during the tafasitamab 60-minute infusion as well as slight tremors for several animals at the control, low-dose and high-dose. The number of animals affected and the number of incidences of tremors were higher at the high-dose. The tremors resolved following the termination of dosing. In addition, statistically significant differences in body temperature
L249: (100-102°F) were observed in males at 100 mg/kg and females at 30 and 100 mg/kg during dosing, that recovered in the recovery period. These data demonstrate tafasitamab-related adverse effects at 100 mg/kg.
L250: ## 5.4.ADME/PK
L251: ## The Applicant's Position:
L252: Following i.v. administration in five separate studies in cynomolgus monkey at dose levels from 0.3 – 100 mg/kg, tafasitamab exhibited a typical 2-compartment PK profile. Tafasitamab showed consistent PK parameters across studies and study groups (volume of distribution at steady state (Vss) 52.6 mL/kg - 124.3 mL/kg, Clearance (CL) 4.31 mL/day/kg - 7.37 mL/day/kg, mean terminal elimination half-life 7.7 days - 14.2 days). Dose linearity was observed between 2-100 mg/kg, and no gender differences were determined. Exposure values (AUC and Cmax) at NOAEL (100mg/kg) were 8-9 fold higher than corresponding values in humans. As expected for a humanized monoclonal antibody in non-human primates, the development of anti-drug antibodies (ADA) was observed, which was associated with increased tafasitamab clearance but not with any other safety relevant laboratory or clinical findings. An additional exploratory PK study in mice was performed confirming the 2-compartment PK profile and demonstrating PK parameters as expected for humanized therapeutic antibodies in mice without TMDD.
L253: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L254: An 8-week

L255: T_{1/2}=10-14 days
L256: Accumulation in C_{max} and AUC was observed during the treatment phase (from the first to the last dose). The percent increase in C_{max} was 26%, 42% and 38% for Groups 2, 3 and 4, respectively, and the percent increase in AUC was 44-46%. Increases in C_{max} and AUC_{0-56} were approximately dose proportional across the dosing range. Immunogenicity: Approximately 47% (14/30) of tafasitamab-treated animals developed ADA responses that were associated with clearing of tafasitamab plasma concentrations. ADA-positive animals with decreased tafasitamab exposure were excluded from the PK analysis.

L268: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L269: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L270: ## The Applicant's Position:
L271: The toxicological assessment was performed in cynomolgus monkeys and included pivotal 8-week (XC-9-14408) and 13-week (MOR208P008) repeat-dose IV infusion toxicity studies. In summary, tafasitamab was well tolerated after weekly IV administration to cynomolgus monkeys at doses up to 100 mg/kg for 13 weeks, with no target organ toxicities or mortalities. Administration of tafasitamab was not associated with electrocardiographic evidence of cardiotoxicity, arrhythmogenesis, or effects on blood pressure when evaluated as part of the pivotal toxicity studies. Importantly, no evidence of an increased susceptibility to infections was noted after tafasitamab administration to cynomolgus monkeys for 13 weeks.
L272: The B-cell clearance observed in these pivotal toxicity studies represents the desired pharmacologic mechanism of action for the proposed therapeutic intervention in B-cell malignancies.
L273: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L274: In general, we agree with the Applicant's assessment; see additional information provided below for the 13-week and 8-week repeat-dose studies.
L275: ## **Study title/ Study number: MOR00208: 13-week intravenous infusion toxicity study in the cynomolgus monkey with a 132 day recovery period/MOR208P008**
L276: ## **Key Study Findings**
L277: - Slight to severe tremors were observed during the tafasitamab infusion. In addition, significant changes were noted in body temperature.
- Tafasitamab caused marked decreases in peripheral blood CD20+ B cells at all doses.
- Tafasitamab caused reductions in T-cell dependent antibody response (TDAR) as assessed by immune response to keyhole limpet hemocyanine (KLH) and Tetanus Toxoid vaccine (TT).
- Lack of germinal centers in lymphoid follicles was observed in several lymphatic organs (spleen, mesenteric and mandibular lymph node) in all tafasitamab-treated groups during the treatment period.
- There were no effects on menstrual cycle length in females and no histopathology/microscopic changes to male and female reproductive organs.
L278: (b) (4)
L279: Conducting laboratory and location:
L280: GLP compliance: Yes

L289: Urinalysis	Unremarkable
Anti-drug-antibodies	Two samples from one animal at 10 mg/kg were confirmed positive for ADA at Week 1 (predose) and Week 18 of the recovery period. There was no impact of ADAs on system exposure or clearance in these animals. It is likely that high levels of the investigational product interfered with the detection of ADAs.
Cytokine Analysis	Female animals of each tafasitamab dose group showed an increase in interleukin-6 (IL-6) values at 4 hours after the end of the infusion for the first dose (Day 1) and the third dose (Day 15). The increase was less pronounced after the third dose.
Immunophenotyping	All doses depleted peripheral B cells (CD3- CD20+) throughout the dosing period; effects were reversible as assessed at the end of the recovery period (Week 19). Absolute NK cells decreased during the first week in several animals, and returned to the range of predose for the rest of the dosing period. Consistent with B-cell depletion observed in peripheral blood, reductions in CD20+ B cells in bone marrow, lymph node and spleen were observed, and were reversible.
